GEODESIC FLOWS, INTERVAL MAPS, 
page=1 block=712609105 (x=53 y=46) width=203 height=6 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
AND SYMBOLIC DYNAMICS 
page=1 block=712609105 (x=78 y=59) width=154 height=6 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
ROY ADLER AND LEOPOLD FLATTO 
page=1 block=1836463382 (x=76 y=82) width=158 height=5 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
1. INTRODUCTION 
page=1 block=1388278453 (x=113 y=123) width=85 height=5 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
Geodesic flows and interval maps are two topics in the theory of 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=14 y=139) width=293 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
dynamical systems with a long mathematical history. The first of 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=152) width=305 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
these seems to have originated with Jacobi who related the flows 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=165) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
to the study of Hamiltonian systems (for a detailed description 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=178) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of the connection,  see [CFS]). The second arises in diverse set­
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=191) width=304 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
tings, such as the modelling of population genetics [Ma] and the 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=203) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
frequency count of digits in continued fraction expansions [Bi]. 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=216) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In both subjects the main problem is to describe the distribution 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=229) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of orbits.  Thus we wish to know how the geodesies spread over 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=242) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
the manifold  containing them and how iterates of points under 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=255) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
an interval map vary over the interval.  Ergodic theory provides 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=268) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
answers to these questions, particularly the notions of ergodicity 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=280) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
and invariant measure which will be elaborated below. 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=293) width=255 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
At first sight the two topics seem unrelated, geodesic flow being a 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=14 y=307) width=295 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
continuous time action and interval map a discrete one. Neverthe­
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=320) width=304 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
less, we shall relate them and their associated symbolic dynamics 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=333) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
when the flow takes place on a compact surface of constant nega­
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=346) width=304 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
tive curvature. In this case we use a graphic approach enabling us 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=359) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
to find a series of reductions from geodesic flow to interval map. 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=2 y=372) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In relating the topics, we show how each sheds light on the other. 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=3 y=384) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
We use ergodicity of interval maps to prove ergodicity of flows and 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=3 y=397) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
conversely. Furthermore, explicit formulas for invariant measures 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=3 y=410) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of interval maps can be derived from invariant measures for flows. 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=3 y=423) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
This fact is interesting as there is a paucity of explicit formulas for 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=3 y=436) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
invariant measures of interval maps. 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=3 y=449) width=172 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
The steps in our reduction scheme are known to exist abstractly. 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=15 y=463) width=294 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
However, for the dynamical systems considered here, the reduc­
page=1 block=934275857 (x=3 y=476) width=304 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
tions are carried out by means of elementary geometry. Our graphic 
page=1 block=934275857 (x=3 y=489) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Received by the editors January 3, 1989 and, in revised form, January 2, 1991.

1991 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 58F11, 58F17.

230 
page=2 block=1818544933 (x=1 y=1) width=15 height=5 fontname=Helvetica wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
ROY ADLER AND LEOPOLD FLATTO 
page=2 block=1793436274 (x=85 y=1) width=138 height=5 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
approach puts into evidence certain facts  not easily  discernible 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=1 y=24) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
otherwise, e.g. the existence of a Markovian partition and of fac­
page=2 block=572868060 (x=1 y=37) width=304 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
tors for the cross section map which we associate with the flow. 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=1 y=50) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
An attractive feature of our graphic approach is that it  renders 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=1 y=63) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
concrete, by means of simple geometric examples, many abstract 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=76) width=305 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
notions of ergodic theory.  However, the method seems intrinsi­
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=88) width=303 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
cally two-dimensional and it is not clear how to extend it to higher 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=101) width=305 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
dimensions. 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=114) width=57 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
We describe in detail the main concepts employed in our work, 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=14 y=127) width=293 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
obtaining at the same time an overview of the paper. 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=140) width=248 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Ergodicity. The ergodic theorem describes the long term average 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=160) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
behavior of certain systems evolving in time.  The theorem has 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=173) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
two versions, a discrete and a continuous one.  We first describe 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=186) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
the discrete one. 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=199) width=79 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Let X  be a measure space—meaning there exists a nonnegative, 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=14 y=212) width=294 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
not identically zero, countably additive measure  m  assigned to 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=225) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
certain subsets of  X 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=238) width=100 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
which are designated as measurable. 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=108 y=238) width=176 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
We 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=291 y=238) width=17 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
assume from now on that all sets under discussion are measurable. 
page=2 block=572868060 (x=2 y=251) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
A transformation  T of X  to itself is measurable if for any set

ROY ADLER AND LEOPOLD FLATTO 
page=4 block=382750013 (x=85 y=1) width=138 height=5 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
FIGURE 1.1. Geodesic flow. 
page=4 block=1240232440 (x=90 y=78) width=134 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Geodesic flows. Let S be a compact  «-dimensional Riemannian 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=5 y=105) width=302 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
manifold and S its unit tangent bundle. For a given vector u e 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=1 y=118) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
S  with base point p e S,  let  y be the unique geodesic passing 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=1 y=131) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
through p and tangent to u. Parametrize y as y(t),  t being arc 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=1 y=144) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
length along y measured from p.  Let Gt(u) be the unit tangent 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=1 y=157) width=306 height=8 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
to y at y{t),  -oo < t < oo, as shown in Figure 1.1. 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=1 y=170) width=245 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
The set of homeomorphisms of S: u —• Gt(u),  -oo < t < oo, is 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=14 y=182) width=300 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
called the geodesic flow on S. For given u, the curve y = Gt(u), 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=2 y=195) width=306 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
-oo < t < oo, is called a flow line or G,-orbit in S (observe that 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=2 y=208) width=305 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
y consists of unit tangents to  y and is to be distinguished from 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=2 y=221) width=305 height=7 fontname=Times-Italic wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
y). 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=2 y=234) width=14 height=7 fontname=Times-Italic wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Liouville's Theorem asserts that the geodesic flow preserves 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=23 y=234) width=285 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
the measure m on S induced on it by the metric of S.  We refer 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=2 y=247) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
to  m  as the Liouville measure and, for  S  of constant negative 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=2 y=260) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
curvature, as the hyperbolic measure. 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=2 y=272) width=176 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In the case where S is of negative curvature, Hedlund and Hopf 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=14 y=286) width=294 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
have shown the geodesic flow to be ergodic [H, Ho]. 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=2 y=299) width=244 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In the sequel we limit ourselves to  S 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=14 y=312) width=183 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
2-dimensional and of 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=204 y=312) width=104 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
constant negative curvature. 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=2 y=325) width=136 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In this case our graphic approach 
page=4 block=489349054 (x=144 y=325) width=164 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
enables us to reduce the subject of geodesicflowsto that of interval

maps.

Historical background. The earlier works on symbolic dynamics

curvature. Artin [A] codes the geodesies on the modular surface

by the continued fraction expansions of the end points, deduc­

ing from this symbolism the existence of periodic and everywhere

GEODESIC FLOWS, INTERVAL MAPS, AND SYMBOLIC DYNAMICS 
page=17 block=592983282 (x=32 y=1) width=244 height=5 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
245 
page=17 block=1448525331 (x=293 y=1) width=13 height=5 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
dense geodesies. The works of Hedlund and Morse [H, Mo], re­
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=24) width=303 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
lating geodesic flows to symbolic sequences, deal exclusively with 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=1 y=37) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
surfaces of genus  g  > 2  defined by a regular  4g-sided  funda­
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=49) width=303 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
mental region with interior angles n/2g. 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=62) width=189 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Hedlund, using an idea 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=198 y=62) width=110 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of Nielsen, represents orbits by a Markovian system; while Morse 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=75) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
achieves a characterization of orbits by sofic systems. 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=88) width=261 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Morse's 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=270 y=88) width=38 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
method was extended to general compact surfaces by Birman and 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=101) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Series [BS]. It is unclear whether Hedlund's method can be likewise 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=114) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
extended. 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=127) width=47 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
The reductions discussed in our paper have previously been 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=14 y=140) width=294 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
given general formulation by various authors. Ambrose and Kaku-
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=153) width=303 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
tani [AK] prove a general representation theorem for measurable 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=166) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
flows as one built over a function and over a transformation. Their 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=179) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
theorem is about the existence of an abstract cross section and cross 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=192) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
section map. The concepts of factor map and natural extension 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=205) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
(see §7) were studied by Rohlin [Ro].  The existence of a sym­
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=217) width=303 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
bolism with Markovian rules was established for geodesicflowson 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=230) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
negative curvature surfaces by Ornstein and Weiss [OW]. Thus the 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=243) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
steps in our reduction scheme are known to exist abstractly from 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=256) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
these works. However in our present work the reduction is made 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=269) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
explicit and has a simple geometric interpretation. 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=282) width=236 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In a series of papers Series [SI-3] also studies the problem of 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=15 y=296) width=293 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
representing geodesic flows by symbolic systems. Comparing her 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=309) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
approach with ours one might say that we have reached more 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=322) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
or less the same mathematical conclusions from opposite direc­
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=334) width=303 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
tions.  Generally speaking, her theorems become our definitions 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=347) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
and vice versa. She first identifies the natural extension Z of the 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=360) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
/-expansions for the Bowen-Series map as the space of modified 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=373) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
cutting sequences (the natural extension Z is defined as the space 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=386) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of two-sided sequences obeying the same admissibility rules as the 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=399) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
/-expansions). She then establishes that the geodesic flow can be 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=411) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
represented as a lowover (£, a), and proves that (Z, a) is con­
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=424) width=304 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
jugate to  (l!,  a'),  where l! 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=2 y=437) width=136 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
is the set of cutting sequences.  In 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=144 y=437) width=165 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
our treatment all this naturally proceeds in the opposite direction. 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=450) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In addition our graphic approach puts into evidence a Markovian 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=463) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
partition leading to Markovian systems rather than to merely sofic 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=476) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
ones, and to formulas for the invariant measure of the Bowen-
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=488) width=304 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Series maps. 
page=17 block=1108924067 (x=3 y=501) width=60 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Comments. To reiterate, the basic ideas of our paper are: we graph 
page=17 block=1984975621 (x=3 y=529) width=308 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
the curvilinear cross section map, straighten out boundaries in this 
page=17 block=1984975621 (x=3 y=542) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Page: 18 left:1 upper:1
246 
page=18 block=348984985 (x=1 y=1) width=14 height=5 fontname=Helvetica-Bold wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
ROY ADLER AND LEOPOLD FLATTO 
page=18 block=2005435445 (x=85 y=1) width=138 height=5 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
graph to obtain the nicer rectilinear map, and establish a conjugacy 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=1 y=24) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
between the maps. In this last act we encounter a discovery which 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=1 y=37) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
may be considered the cornerstone of our work—namely "bulges 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=1 y=50) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
fit into corners" (see Theorem 5.1 and Figures 5.3, 5.6). All other 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=1 y=63) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
ideas in the paper follow naturally from this basic observation. 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=1 y=76) width=294 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Our treatment of geodesic flow is based on simple geometric 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=13 y=88) width=294 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
ideas, but unfortunately, these carry along with them a quantity of 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=1 y=101) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
forbidding detail. We are confronted with the problem of putting 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=1 y=114) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
geometrical ideas into words ("one picture is worth a thousand 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=1 y=127) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
words provided one uses another thousand words to justify the 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=1 y=140) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
picture" [St, p. 225]). Another difficulty stems from the fact that 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=1 y=153) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
we describe all orbits in the flow. Removal of a set of flow lines 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=1 y=165) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of measure zero—namely the flow lines of S passing through the 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=2 y=178) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
intersection points of the curves in B = \J y.—would have simpli­
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=2 y=191) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
fied the analysis considerably just as in [AF2]; but then we would 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=2 y=204) width=309 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
have failed to account for all orbits. 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=2 y=217) width=170 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
We remark that the concepts of this paper—i.e. the introduction 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=14 y=231) width=295 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of the rectilinear map and its conjugacy to the curvilinear one— 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=2 y=244) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
can be applied to any fundamental region satisfying the extension 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=2 y=257) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
condition. We have limited our discussion to the  (8# - 4)-sided 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=2 y=269) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
regions of Theorem 1.1 for two reasons: 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=2 y=282) width=189 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
1. Universality—such a region exists for all surface groups  T. 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=15 y=295) width=291 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
2. Simplicity—in this case the rectilinear domain has the sim­
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=14 y=308) width=292 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
plest possible description, traceable to the fact that the number of 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=2 y=321) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
fundamental regions meeting at a vertex equals 4, which is the 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=2 y=334) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
smallest possible number when the extension property holds. 
page=18 block=1939990953 (x=2 y=347) width=284 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Summary. We summarize the contents of the various sections. 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=2 y=373) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Our work relies on various results which seem all but impossible 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=2 y=386) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
to reference.  To assist the reader, we include several appendices 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=2 y=399) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
providing the proofs of these results. 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=3 y=412) width=174 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In §2 we introduce some background in hyperbolic geometry 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=15 y=425) width=294 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
with notation necessary for our discussion of geodesic flows. 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=3 y=438) width=288 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In §3 we introduce the (8g - 4)-sided fundamental polygon F. 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=15 y=451) width=294 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
We use F to read off a set of generators for T and a set of group 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=3 y=464) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
relations these satisfy.  The relations are used to establish conju-
page=18 block=119358627 (x=3 y=477) width=304 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
ugacy between the curvilinear and rectilinear maps introduced in 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=3 y=489) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
§4.5. 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=3 y=502) width=25 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In §4 we define the cross section  C  and its associated cross 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=15 y=515) width=295 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
section map Tc . C is coordinatized. The coordinate description 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=3 y=528) width=307 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of Tc,  also designated by  Tc,  is called the curvilinear map. The 
page=18 block=119358627 (x=3 y=541) width=307 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Page: 19 left:1 upper:1
GEODESIC FLOWS, INTERVAL MAPS, AND SYMBOLIC DYNAMICS 
page=19 block=2073621255 (x=32 y=1) width=244 height=5 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
247 
page=19 block=1208121709 (x=293 y=1) width=13 height=5 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
dynamics of Tc  is difficult to understand. To remedy the situation 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=1 y=23) width=306 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
we introduce in §5 a second map TR , called rectilinear, and show 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=1 y=36) width=307 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
that  Tc  and  TR are conjugate. 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=1 y=49) width=151 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In §6 we show that the Bowen-Series maps—the factors of  7^ 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=14 y=62) width=293 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
and TRl—are ergodic. We also derive formulas for the invariant 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=72) width=306 height=12 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
measures of these maps. Then in §7 we show how the ergodicity 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=88) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of the Bowen-Series maps imply the ergodicity of the flow  Gt. 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=101) width=293 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In §8 we obtain the admissibility rules for the rectilinear se­
page=19 block=873610597 (x=14 y=116) width=291 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
quences. The symbolic sequences for  TR are obtained by splicing 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=129) width=306 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
the  /-expansions of the factors of  TR  and  TRl. 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=139) width=235 height=12 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
The splicing 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=248 y=142) width=60 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
assumes somewhat different  forms depending as to whether we 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=155) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
use Markovian or sofic sequences. In §9 we obtain geometric in­
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=168) width=304 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
terpretations of both curvilinear and rectilinear sequences. These 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=181) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
interpretations lead to rules for coding curvilinear sequences to 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=194) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
rectilinear ones. 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=207) width=76 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In §10 we discuss the main unresolved questions suggested by 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=14 y=222) width=294 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
our work. 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=235) width=48 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In Appendix A we prove the existence of the (8#-4)-sided poly­
page=19 block=873610597 (x=14 y=248) width=294 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
gon. The proof is based on two theorems which we leave unproved 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=261) width=308 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
but for which we provide findable references: namely, a theorem 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=274) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of Poincaré giving conditions for a finite sided polygon to be a 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=287) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
fundamental region for a discrete group acting on the hyperbolic 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=299) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
plane, and the theorem of Fenchel-Nielsen on extending homeo-
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=312) width=304 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
morphisms between universal covering spaces to their boundaries. 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=325) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
For another approach, more direct but somewhat lengthier, see 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=338) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Stillwell's appendix in [D, pp. 379-386]. 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=3 y=351) width=191 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In Appendix B we prove the folklore theorem and its converse. 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=15 y=364) width=294 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
The converse seems to be a new addition to the subject, and we 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=2 y=377) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
make use of it to prove ergodicity of Bowen-Series maps. 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=3 y=390) width=268 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In Appendix C, we discuss symbolic systems. We give several 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=15 y=403) width=294 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
definitions of Markovian and sofic systems, illustrating these with 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=3 y=416) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
examples. We then develop the theory of Markov partitions in a 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=3 y=429) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
manner more basic and elementary than found in the literature. 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=3 y=442) width=299 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Finally, in Appendix D, we present an alternate proof to The­
page=19 block=873610597 (x=15 y=455) width=292 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
orem 9.7 of §9, which provides further  insight to the interplay 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=3 y=468) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
between geometry and the combinatorics of symbolism. 
page=19 block=873610597 (x=3 y=481) width=262 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5

of st,  to aa(* , the forward end point of s~^ . Similar reasoning 
page=29 block=989892772 (x=1 y=44) width=307 height=8 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
applies to the remaining points. 
page=29 block=989892772 (x=1 y=58) width=150 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
4.  CROSS SECTION 
page=29 block=1020520290 (x=112 y=82) width=85 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
To study the geodesic flow Vt  on S we introduce the notion of 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=13 y=97) width=294 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
a cross section map. Roughly speaking, a cross section is a subset 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=1 y=110) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of S which every G,-orbit hits again and again, both in the future 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=123) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
and in the past. The correspondence between successive points of 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=136) width=305 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
return to the cross section serves to define the cross section map. 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=149) width=303 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Let B consist of the  ^-projections of vectors tangent to  5F. 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=14 y=162) width=294 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
B is the union of  2g  closed  G,-orbits.  B and  S0 = S - B are 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=175) width=306 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
both  G,-invariant, so that the study of the flow on S reduces to 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=1 y=188) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
the study of the flow on S0 . As a preliminary choice for our cross 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=201) width=306 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
section, we choose Cp to consist of the ^-projections of vectors 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=211) width=306 height=10 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
with base point in  OF and pointing into the interior of one of 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=227) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
the fundamental regions bordering on F. Referring to Figure 1.7, 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=240) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
B is the set of vectors tangent to the system of curves {y.} and 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=253) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Cp is the set of vectors not tangent to but with base point in that 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=263) width=307 height=10 fontname=Times-Italic wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
system. Because of the pairing of edges of F, every element in Cp 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=276) width=305 height=10 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
is also a H projection of a vector based on dF  and pointing into 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=292) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
F. The geodesic determined by the latter eventually leaves F and 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=305) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
thus it is clear that Cp is a cross section. 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=2 y=315) width=194 height=9 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
In order to obtain a simple coordinate description of the cross 
page=29 block=530653666 (x=14 y=332) width=295 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
section map, we add to  Cp  the seemingly artificial sets Cn  de­

scribed below. The enlarged cross section C consists of the sets

We remark that Series [S2, p. 355] proves the same result by 
page=45 block=225344427 (x=14 y=167) width=294 height=6 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
a completely different method. Her proof has the interesting fea­
page=45 block=225344427 (x=2 y=179) width=304 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
ture that it involves the interior of the disk, whereas the maps  ƒ 
page=45 block=225344427 (x=2 y=192) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
and g are defined only on the boundary. The idea is as follows. 
page=45 block=225344427 (x=2 y=205) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Iterates of the map are piecewise equal to motions—i.e., Möbius 
page=45 block=225344427 (x=2 y=218) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
transformations mapping ED onto itself—specified by words in the

generators. 
page=45 block=225344427 (x=2 y=244) width=52 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Upon iteration these motions map a central funda­
page=45 block=225344427 (x=60 y=244) width=246 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
mental region to ones which are, in the Euclidean metric, expo­
page=45 block=225344427 (x=2 y=257) width=304 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
nentially small and close to the boundary of the disk. Intervals on 
page=45 block=225344427 (x=2 y=270) width=309 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
the boundary in a basic partition refined by iteration of the map 
page=45 block=225344427 (x=2 y=283) width=310 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
are related to fundamental regions in the interior, and this relation 
page=45 block=225344427 (x=2 y=296) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
forces these intervals also to be exponentially small. Expansiveness 
page=45 block=225344427 (x=2 y=309) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
is then a simple consequence of this fact. 
page=45 block=225344427 (x=2 y=322) width=194 height=6 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5

We conclude this section with some remarks comparing the 
page=59 block=1636050357 (x=14 y=127) width=295 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
above Markovian and sofic expansions.  Sofic admissibility rules 
page=59 block=1636050357 (x=2 y=140) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
are more difficult than Markovian ones which have the virtue of 
page=59 block=1636050357 (x=2 y=153) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
being one-step rules. However, a price must be paid in the Marko­
page=59 block=1636050357 (x=2 y=166) width=304 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
vian case. In order to get a TR-expansion of (£, rj) e R by splicing 
page=59 block=1636050357 (x=1 y=179) width=315 height=8 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
an /-expansion of Ç with a ^-expansion of rj, we cannot use the 
page=59 block=1636050357 (x=2 y=191) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
ordinary ^-expansion and must modify the notion. The sofic case 
page=59 block=1636050357 (x=2 y=204) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
is easier in this respect as the ordinary  ^-expansion will do. The 
page=59 block=1636050357 (x=2 y=217) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Markovian case has an additional difficulty over the sofic one. To

EPILOGUE 
page=70 block=171493374 (x=140 y=492) width=48 height=5 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
Our study has been restricted to compact surfaces because for 
page=70 block=1876682596 (x=13 y=504) width=294 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
surface groups we have the existence of a fundamental region satis­
page=70 block=1876682596 (x=1 y=517) width=304 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
fying the crucial extension condition. It might be interesting to see

In this appendix we prove the existence of the (Sg -  4)-sided 
page=72 block=20853837 (x=14 y=386) width=294 height=6 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
fundamental  polygon described in Theorem 3.1.

The proof is

the theory of Fuchsian groups. We do not state these theorems in

their entirety, and quote only those parts used to prove Theorem 
page=72 block=20853837 (x=2 y=438) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
3.1.

Poincaré's theorem gives sufficient conditions for a polygon to

be a fundamental region of a Fuchsian group. Let F be a bounded

polygon with an even number of consecutive edges sx, ... , sn ori­
page=72 block=20853837 (x=2 y=489) width=304 height=8 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
ented in the counter-clockwise  direction, and let pl9  ...  9pn  be 
page=72 block=20853837 (x=3 y=502) width=305 height=8 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
the respective initial points of these edges. Assume that st, 
page=72 block=20853837 (x=3 y=515) width=276 height=8 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
s,t),

1 < i < n, are of equal hyperbolic length, where  a(i)  is a given 
page=72 block=20853837 (x=4 y=528) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
permutation of 1, ... , n of order two without fixed elements. Let

APPENDIX  B.  THE  FOLKLORE THEOREM 
page=74 block=121167003 (x=64 y=463) width=184 height=5 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
This appendix deals with  a topic that  we call the  "Folklore 
page=74 block=1014486152 (x=15 y=478) width=294 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
theorem." 
page=74 block=1014486152 (x=3 y=491) width=48 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
It is not clear to us to whom it should be credited. 
page=74 block=1014486152 (x=58 y=491) width=251 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
One could justifiably  attribute it to Renyi [R]. His theorem has 
page=74 block=1014486152 (x=3 y=504) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
the desired conclusions: yet his work is so heavily influenced by 
page=74 block=1014486152 (x=3 y=517) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
the classical presentation of continued fractions that not enough 
page=74 block=1014486152 (x=3 y=530) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
emphasis is placed on the fact  that his theorem  is really about 
page=74 block=1014486152 (x=4 y=543) width=305 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5

We endow the sequence space X^ with a topology so that no­
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=14 y=167) width=292 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5

page=83 block=2081652693 (x=2 y=180) width=309 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5

page=83 block=2081652693 (x=2 y=193) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5

page=83 block=2081652693 (x=2 y=206) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
topological are often more efficient. 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=2 y=219) width=168 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
We define the distance between two distinct sequences to be 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=14 y=232) width=295 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
l/(|/i| + l) where n is the coordinate of smallest modulus at which 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=3 y=245) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
they differ. This means that the more two sequences agree around 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=2 y=258) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
the central coordinate the closer they are. Some elementary conse­
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=2 y=271) width=304 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
quences of this distance function is that X^ is a compact metric 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=2 y=284) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
space (the Cantor discontinuum), the shift a homeomorphism, and 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=2 y=297) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
finite cylinder sets—i.e. sets of sequences where afiniteset of coor­
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=1 y=310) width=309 height=6 fontname=Times-Italic wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
dinates are specified—are closed-open sets. There are a countable 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=2 y=323) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
number of these cylinder sets and they generate the topology. Fur­
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=2 y=336) width=305 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
thermore, any closed-open subset is compact and hence the union 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=2 y=349) width=307 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of a finite number of finite cylinder sets. 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=3 y=362) width=190 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
A subshift (X, a) is defined as a shift-invariant subspace X of 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=15 y=374) width=294 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
some  X^  together with the restriction to X of  a.

page=83 block=2081652693 (x=3 y=400) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
combinatorial description of X is as follows. The complement of 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=3 y=413) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
X is also shift invariant and is a countable union of finite cylinder 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=3 y=426) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
sets. Without loss of generality we can assume that the cylinder

sets in question are specified by consecutive coordinates. We call

plus a location specifies such a finite cylinder set. However, with­

page=83 block=2081652693 (x=3 y=491) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
finite cylinder set. Thus a countable shift-invariant union of finite 
page=83 block=2081652693 (x=3 y=504) width=313 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
cylinder sets corresponds to a countable list of initefc-blocks.We

(Z( ), a),  which is

following  1-step admissibility rules: one A>block follows another

if and only if

We can consider a larger more complicated class of dynamical 
page=88 block=808228639 (x=14 y=306) width=294 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5

page=88 block=808228639 (x=2 y=319) width=303 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5

page=88 block=808228639 (x=2 y=331) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5

shift.  Thus one can view a sofic system as the shift acting on the 
page=88 block=808228639 (x=2 y=370) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
space of bi-infinite walks on a directed graph, the vertices (edges) 
page=88 block=808228639 (x=2 y=383) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of which are not necessarily distinctly labeled.  SFT's are clearly 
page=88 block=808228639 (x=2 y=396) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
sofic systems. But the converse is not true. A sofic system which 
page=88 block=808228639 (x=2 y=409) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
is not a SFT is referred to as strictly sofic.

Theorem C6. The two definitions of sofic are equivalent. 
page=89 block=897848096 (x=2 y=329) width=275 height=7 fontname=Times-Italic wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
Proof. According to the first, we can assume as we have done be­

the sofic system are just the images of follower sets in the topolog­

page=89 block=897848096 (x=3 y=435) width=310 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
number of these as well. Therefore, the first definition implies the 
page=89 block=897848096 (x=3 y=448) width=307 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
second.

s_x) = (t_n, ... , t_x).  A strictly sofic system is, therefore, one for

Application to abstract dynamical systems 
page=91 block=2144665602 (x=23 y=41) width=194 height=7 fontname=Times-Bold wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
lia. The fundamental complication. The pair (X, T), where T is 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=1 y=59) width=313 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
a mapping of a space X  onto itself, is called an abstract dynam­
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=1 y=72) width=304 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
ical system. Additional topological and measurability structure is 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=85) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
usually assumed. A symbolic representation of such a dynamical 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=98) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
system is a useful tool in understanding it.  By symbolic repre­
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=111) width=303 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
sentation we mean that  (X,  T)  is a factor of a subshift 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=124) width=269 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
(Z, a) 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=277 y=124) width=31 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
under a homomorphism  n. 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=137) width=134 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
However, representing an abstract 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=144 y=137) width=164 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
dynamical system by a symbolic one involves a fundamental com­
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=149) width=303 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
plication. We have two desires: we would like a continuous one-
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=162) width=303 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
to-one correspondence between orbits  Tnx 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=172) width=199 height=10 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of the first and orbits 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=207 y=175) width=101 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
ff%.. 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=188) width=27 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
, s__x, s0, sx, ... ) of the second; and we want  Z to be a 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=35 y=188) width=275 height=8 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
closed set, preferably a subshift of finite type. Unfortunately these 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=201) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
two desires are in conflict: constraints placed by topology must be 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=214) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
observed.  On the one hand a continuous one-to-one correspon­
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=226) width=303 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
dence makes X  homeomorphic to S. On the other hand X is to­
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=239) width=304 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
tally disconnected while X  is often a smooth or piecewise smooth 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=252) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
manifold. 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=265) width=47 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
By sacrificing one-to-one correspondence we can still 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=55 y=265) width=253 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
salvage a satisfactory symbolization of orbits. This is reminiscent 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=278) width=306 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
of the familiar situation in arithmetic:  namely, certain rational 
page=91 block=1396431506 (x=2 y=291) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
numbers have two decimal expansions.

first is continuous, the second merely piecewise so. The second, a

slight variation of the first, exhibits a further complication to be

resolved.

titions gotten from Markov ones by amalgamation of elements give

rise to sofic systems. We call partitions gotten in this manner sofic. 
page=99 block=1848125895 (x=2 y=373) width=309 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5

page=99 block=1848125895 (x=2 y=386) width=304 height=7 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
finement. The coarse partition of §8 for the rectilinear map is an 
page=99 block=1848125895 (x=2 y=399) width=306 height=6 fontname=Times-Roman wordint.min=3 wordint.max=5
example of a sofic one.
